4.4.2    Crop model simulations [SOURCE: Page_110]

The objective of the calibration procedure is to link a Mitscherlich Baule function to each farm-type in order to capture the response of maize yield to both nitrogen and water. As in the single-input method, the response functions considered in this exercise are obtained using the STICS crop model. The approach for designing simulated experiments and producing maize yield response functions with STICS is similar to the original method by Godard et al. (2008), and we therefore use soil data from the European Soil Database (ESDB) 4 and daily climate information from the database of the Monitoring Agriculture from Remote Sensing (MARS) project 5 . [SOURCE: Page_110]

More precisely, for each of the three altitude classes found in FADN we carried out 800 simulations (for a total of 2,400) per region, corresponding to all possible combinations of (i) the ten most prevalent soil types according to the ESD ; (ii) 16 possible maize varieties 4. http ://eusoils.jrc.ec.europa.eu/esdb_archive/ESDB/Index.htm/. 5. http ://www.marsop.info/marsop3/. [SOURCE: Page_110]

110                                                                                          4.4. Illustrative example [SOURCE: Page_111]

which were parameterized in STICS using expert knowledge and differ as regards earliness and yield potential, and (iii) five sowing dates derived from estimates based on the work of Willekens et al. (1998) (see Leclère et al. (2013) for more information). By comparison, Godard et al. (2008) considered only 30 or 60 combinations to create the universe of all possible response functions, depending on whether the simulated crop was irrigated (Table 4.1). The obvious advantage of the larger number of simulations that we carry out in this paper is that we increase considerably the probability of finding a function that achieves a finer calibration. [SOURCE: Page_111]

TABLE 4.1 – Summary of combinations in creating yield response curves [SOURCE: Page_111]

Modality per crop Data input options Godard method ∗    Current method Climate                               1                         1 Soil                                  5                        10 Crop varieties #                    3 or 1                     16 Sowing date #                       1 or 3                      5 Irrigation                          1 or 2            treated as a variable Preceding crop                        2                         1 Altitude class                        1                         3 Total combinations                 30 or 60                     2,400 ∗ Source : Godard et al. (2008). # Depending on the crop, these two options create a total of 3 combinations. [SOURCE: Page_111]

As in the case of nitrogen, the use of a crop model to simulate the response of yields to water inputs requires knowledge of the timing of the irrigation applications. Unlike fer- tilization, however, an appropriate irrigation schedule cannot be easily specified prior to model simulations because the timing depends on (i) water availability and (ii) the climate data used in the simulation, namely the rainfall distribution and the evaporating power of the atmosphere which defines the daily crop water requirements. Therefore, to generate the set of points that leads to the estimation of a response function, we followed a simple approach involving an initial run in which the model automatically calculated the day and the optimal amount of water applied to the field. We define an "optimal" irrigation schedule as one that satisfies two requirements. The first is that irrigation must take place when the wa- [SOURCE: Page_111]

111                                                                    4.4. Illustrative example [SOURCE: Page_112]

ter stress index falls below unity. The second is that irrigation must fully cover any soil water deficit, so as to restore soil water content to field capacity. Both requirements are satisfied by STICS, since the model provides an option for automated irrigation by defining the value of the water stress index below which irrigation is carried out. An iterative procedure is then implemented in the following STICS runs, so that irrigation takes place on the same dates as in the initial run, but with continuously decreasing amounts of water, causing accumulation of water stress and thus reducing yields. [SOURCE: Page_112]

For each of the 2,400 possible combinations of the different modalities, a total of 121 model runs was performed, corresponding to 11 levels of nitrogen (between 0-600 kg/ha using a 60-kg step) multiplied by 11 levels of irrigation (between 0-100% of the optimal amount, per step of 10%, at 0.7 efficiency). Every set of points in the yield-water-nitrogen space produced by these 121 iterative simulations in STICS was then fitted to a MB function using non-linear least squares. [SOURCE: Page_112]

Although the previous method is easy to implement and has already been used for similar purposes (e.g. Larson et al., 1996; Kampas et al., 2012), we need to acknowledge that in reality farmers facing water scarcity constraints are forced to apply various management strategies to stabilize yields, involving appropriate adjustments to the irrigation schedule (FAO, 2012). For example, deficit irrigation is a practice that aims at maximizing water use efficiency by carefully selecting the timing of applications, given a limited amount of irrigation water and uncertain precipitation patterns, and requires very good knowledge of the effects of water stress on the different crop growth phases (FAO, 2002). The literature suggests that simulating deficit irrigation with crop models is actually a trial and error procedure in which the irrigation dates need to be modified according to the total amount of water considered in the experiment. This approach ensures that water stress in critical crop growth phases is minimized and it can be considered as the optimal water management practice for a given amount of available water (García-Vila et al., 2009). Nevertheless, due to the computational complexity of the exercise (a trial and error procedure would be required for [SOURCE: Page_112]

112                                                                   4.4. Illustrative example [SOURCE: Page_113]

each of the 2,400 simulations in every region) we have opted not to simulate deficit irrigation for constructing water-yield response functions. [SOURCE: Page_113]

An alternative to defining specific irrigation dates and reducing the amount of water applied in each of them would be to simulate yield response for different critical values of the water stress index. Although this approach has the advantage that the water application dates are automatically estimated by the crop model, it does not allow the modeler to control the total amount of water used in the experiment ; a low critical value of the water stress index leads to fewer applications, but with larger amounts of water, than in the case of high critical values of the water stress index, since an irrigation application is now triggered by a higher soil water deficit. Preliminary work involving simulation experiments not reported here have actually resulted in almost vertical (step-wise) response functions, even when the maximum amount of water per application was controlled for. [SOURCE: Page_113]

4.4.3    Dealing with missing input prices [SOURCE: Page_113]

From the presentation of the selection criterion it becomes clear that the unitary prices of nitrogen and water are two key parameters for the calibration procedure. Given that a farmer may be facing availability or institutional constraints, cN and cW actually represent the total cost paid by the farmer to access both inputs. For example, cW includes the water charges paid to the local authorities (if any), the energy costs related to the irrigation tech- nology, additional implicit costs as a result of water availability problems and also possible transaction costs (Gómez-Limón and Martinez, 2006). The above discussion suggests that the unitary input prices, as perceived by a farmer in his decision problem, are not necessarily equal to their respective market prices, although they may be strongly correlated if a market for these inputs actually exists. Nevertheless, even in the case of established markets, such price information is impossible to retrieve from databases such as FADN, since all costs are aggregated at the farm level. Note that, although this discussion applies mainly for water, a [SOURCE: Page_113]

113                                                                   4.4. Illustrative example [SOURCE: Page_114]

unitary price for nitrogen is also difficult to estimate because of the multiple types of chemi- cal fertilizers with varying contents of mineral nitrogen used for any given crop by different farms in the same region. Furthermore, cN may also include additional implicit costs related to policy constraints on the use of fertilizers. [SOURCE: Page_114]

To circumvent the above information problem, instead of assuming a specific input price, we use a reasonable range of values for cN and cW , in accordance with all available information obtained from expert opinion and literature sources. Thus we calculate the selection criterion λ for each estimated function (corresponding to a specific parameter set θ ) and for each combination of potential cW and cN prices. The range of nitrogen prices was created around an average value of 1.89 A C/kg (plus or minus 20%, by step of 5%), which was based on unpublished estimates produced by the European project FoodSecure 6 (Stéphane De Cara, personal communication, October 6, 2015). [SOURCE: Page_114]

C/m3 (at a step of 0.01 A The selected range for water prices was 0–0.2 A                       C/m3 ) which is comparable to similar prices found in the literature. For example, Bartolini et al. (2007) report C/m3 for various crops and regions in Italy. This is confirmed by water prices of 0.016–0.31 A C/m3 Dono et al. (2010) who estimate water pumping (electricity) costs from 0.026 to 0.091 A C/m3 paid to the local water agency. In for Sardinia and also report a water fee of 0.0301 A C/m3 for their analysis of water demand in Greece, Kampas et al. (2012) use a range 0–0.2 A Thessaly. For the French water basin of Beauce, part of which belongs to the Centre region C/m3 , considered in this article, Graveline and Mérel (2014) report a total water cost of 0.055 A C/m3 , paid to the basin authority. As regards to the resource cost including a fee of 0.014 A of water, which is also assumed to be included in cW , Graveline et al. (2012) estimate mean C/m3 for the Alsace and Midi-Pyrénées marginal water prices in the range of 0.055–0.082 A C/m3 regions in France, Kampas et al. (2014) estimate a resource cost range of 0.083–0.195 A for Thessaly, Greece, while Gomez-Limon J. et al. (2004) report prices from 0.01 to 0.11 C/m3 for achieving full cost recovery in Spain. 114                                                                                 4.5. Results [SOURCE: Page_115]

4.5     Results [SOURCE: Page_115]

Crop model simulations with varying amounts of nitrogen and water and for different combinations of soil, crop variety and management options resulted in 2,400 sets of data points in the yield-nitrogen-water space, which in turn produced an equivalent number of MB response functions for each region using nonlinear least squares regression. We thus estimate five parameters (the elements of set θ = {φ , σ , τ,W , N }) and the large number of data points (121 combinations of nitrogen and water) seem to fit very well to the MB equation as can be seen by the high R$_2$ presented in Table 4.2. This reveals that the growth engine of the STICS crop model tends to produce exponential yield response to both inputs. [SOURCE: Page_115]

In order to associate each farm-type with a single maize response function we "fil- tered" the set of all response functions C(θ ) and for each farm-type we focus on those functions for which the estimated attainable maize yield (φ ) is higher than the observed y . Criterion λ is then calculated for all remaining response functions in each farm-type and for different water and nitrogen prices. Finally, we selected the response function which returns the lowest λ value. Table 4.2 presents the results for all farm-types in the regions of Centre and Aquitaine. [SOURCE: Page_115]

The second column of Table 4.2 shows the percentage of irrigated maize area in each farm-type, while the y∗ yield column in Table 4.2 represents the result of the profit maximization problem under no yield constraints and is used here as an alternative measure of validation for the proposed calibration criterion ; the discrepancy between observed yield y and the optimal yield y∗ for the selected set of input prices (cW , cN ) illustrates the importance of finding a function for which λ is as close to zero as possible (see farm-type 20, region Centre). More precisely, a high | λ | value corresponds to a selected response function for which the surface gradient is not perfectly collinear to the price vector. As a result, the optimal point on the yield surface when profit is maximized will be different from [SOURCE: Page_115]

115                   4.5. Results [SOURCE: Page_116]

the observed yield. [SOURCE: Page_116]

117                                                                                  4.5. Results [SOURCE: Page_118]

Figure 4.3 presents the final selected response surfaces for nine indicative farm-types in Centre and Aquitaine, and also plots the two vectors that need to be collinear to achieve calibration. It is clear from this figure that for farm-type 20 in Centre, the two vectors are far from being collinear and the resulting low quality of calibration is also confirmed by the high λ values and the discrepancy between the simulated and the reference yields (y∗ and y ) in Table 4.2. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Although a low quality of calibration is not a problem in itself and can be possibly addressed by increasing the number of simulations carried out, it is obvious that the esti- mated W ∗ is generally lower as the irrigation percentage decreases. This can be seen more clearly in Figure 4.4 which shows the added irrigation water in relation to the average irri- gated area for maize in the same farm-type. Since, as mentioned above, the AROPAj model does not distinguish between irrigated and rainfed crops, the reference yield y tends to be lower as the share of irrigated maize decreases. This leads to a greater theoretical yield gap dy = φ − y and as a result, the MB function suggests that y can be attained with few water and nitrogen inputs. This is the reason why the derived W ∗ (and N ∗ in some cases) seems to be unrealistically low, although the calibration is considered successful, given the small | λ | value and the numerical proximity between y∗ and y in most farm-types. [SOURCE: Page_118]

The previous discussion suggests that the estimated added irrigation water W ∗ would be higher if only irrigated maize was considered in the illustrative example. A rough norma- lization that involves dividing W ∗ with the irrigated share of maize (results not presented here) indeed raises W ∗ for maize to the 1,600-2,700 m per ha range, reported by the French national statistics for the period 2003-2006 (Rabaud and Chassard, 2007). Graveline and Mérel (2014) also report average water usage around 2,000 m /ha for maize in the Centre region. [SOURCE: Page_118]

Interestingly, in farm-types with rainfed maize (20 and 24 for Centre and 17 and 22 for Aquitaine) the reference yield y is at a similar level, or even higher, than other farm-types in which the crop is irrigated. As a result, in order to account for zero added water (W ∗ = ), [SOURCE: Page_118]

118                                                                                 4.5. Results [SOURCE: Page_119]

the σ slope parameter in the selected MB functions takes on higher values indicating a more sensitive response to the physical water inputs W . The economic intuition behind these high σ values is a potentially greater yield variability depending on the weather conditions, and hence higher economic risk since water inputs are not controlled by the farmer. At the same time, σ exhibits higher variation than the nitrogen-related slope parameter τ ; the respective coefficients of variation are 1.750 and 0.428 for Centre, and 1.715 and 0.524 for Aquitaine. This result is expected since water constitutes an agronomic input whose marginal physical productivity depends greatly on the water holding capacity of the soil, the designed irrigation schedule and the temporal distribution of rainfall. This means that the effect of water inputs (physical or through irrigation) on crop yields across different farm-types is expected, in theory, to show more variability that of nitrogen. The same discussion also applies for parameter W , which shows more variability than the respective parameter N representing the amount of soil nitrogen not originating from chemical fertilizers. [SOURCE: Page_119]

The selection procedure also provides an estimation of the amounts of nitrogen and irrigation water used by the different farm-types. Thus the mean amount of mineral nitro- gen used amounts to 74.8 kg/ha for Centre and 107.1 kg/ha for Aquitaine. The respective amounts of irrigation water are 489 m^3 /ha and 750.8 m^3 /ha. Contrary to the input quantities, the distribution of input prices is relatively similar for both regions ; the mean water price is C/m^3 for Centre and 0.089 A estimated at 0.114 A                         C/m^3 for Aquitaine, whereas the mean cost of nitrogen is equal to 1.94 A C/kg for both regions. [SOURCE: Page_119]

One basic assumption of both the standard "Godard" method and its extension to two inputs presented in this paper is that a single soil type, variety and sowing date corresponds to a maize production system in a specific farm-type. Hence, among the ESDB’s ten most common soils in each region, which were used for the creation of the set of response func- tions, the most frequently selected type for Centre was a medium soil characterized by a clay content of 25% (soils #1 and #2 in Table A2 in the appendix). Interestingly, this soil type is also the most common type found in the region in terms of land area. In Aquitaine, the most [SOURCE: Page_119]

119                                                                               4.5. Results [SOURCE: Page_120]

FIGURE 4.3 – Example of calibrated response functions for maize in the Centre and Aqui- taine regions. Each Mitscherlich-Baule response function is associated with one farm-type (a virtual farm representing a number of real farms of similar technical and economic characte- ristics). Yield is a function of nitrogen and water and its isoquant at the observed level for 2009 (the reference year) is colored green. The red vector represents the input prices and the black vector represents the gradient to the surface (both vectors were set at a different length for better visualization). [SOURCE: Page_120]

frequently selected soil type is also the most common type according to the ESDB (soil #1). It is a fine soil which is typically found around the rivers of the area and has a clay content of 45%, a limestone content of 5% and a higher pH than the prevalent soil in the region Centre. [SOURCE: Page_120]

Among the 16 maize varieties considered, the one most frequently selected one for Centre was an intermediate variety as regards earliness and yield potential (see Table A3 in the appendix for details on maize varieties). For Aquitaine, which is located in the southern part of France, the most frequently selected varieties were intermediate ones (varieties #3, #4, #15) or precocious ones (varieties #1, #9, #11, #13, #14). No late variety was chosen, which is not surprising as the climate, although warm, still doesn’t allow the [SOURCE: Page_120]

As regards sowing dates, the majority of the selected response functions correspond to an average sowing date for maize in both regions. [SOURCE: Page_121]

4.6                           Discussion and Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_121]

In this paper we present a method for calibrating two-input response functions against observed economic data from the FADN database, in order to better represent the bio- physical aspects of production in MP bio-economic models. The variable inputs considered are irrigation water and nitrogen. The procedure is based on the single-input calibration method developed by Godard et al. (2008) and consists of selecting an appropriate response [SOURCE: Page_121]

121                                                            4.6. Discussion and Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_122]

function among the large set of alternatives produced by means of a crop model. The main assumption of the calibration method is that the selected response function evaluated at the observed yield level for a reference year is the one that best approximates the optimality conditions of the farm’s profit maximization problem. Because yield is a function of two inputs and represents an isoquant in the input space, the approximation criterion relies on the collinearity relationship between the slope gradient of the yield surface and the vector of price ratios. It is clear that the proposed method does not guarantee finding a function that fully satisfies the calibration criterion (perfect collinearity between the vectors). Nevertheless, the method provides functions that appear to fit very well the corresponding optimality conditions for a given set of parameters (yields and costs). The method is also able to infer the physical quantities of inputs used, a major modeling advantage when such information is not available to the analyst. [SOURCE: Page_122]

The algebraic representation of the calibration criterion is a function of input and crop prices and also depends on the shadow prices of the constraints in the farmer’s profit maximization program. Because of the uncertainty pertaining to most of these parameters, especially the price of water, we carry out a simulation exercise by considering a specific range of input prices, defined according to existing information. Our illustrative example for maize in 22 farm-types from two administrative regions in France reveals that the calibration procedure produces satisfactory results in most cases. [SOURCE: Page_122]

In interpreting the results we need to take into account the different sources of bias that are inherent to the calibration procedure and may affect the estimation of the amounts of inputs used. The initial source of bias comes from the specification of the functional source itself ; although the MB has been shown to generally represent yield response to inputs generally accurately, it may not be always the best choice for certain crops. For example, Amon-Armah et al. (2015) compared the MB with quadratic functions to represent the res- ponse of yield to nitrogen fertilization and found that the polynomial form best represented corn, wheat and barley while MB best represented potato. This bias is a trade-off for the [SOURCE: Page_122]

122                                                            4.6. Discussion and Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_123]

convenient mathematical properties of the MB function, namely the existence of a plateau, the continuous derivatives and the substitutability between inputs. Nevertheless, the results from the simulations with the STICS crop model suggest an exponential response to changes in the level of input use which justifies the choice of the MB function, although the validation of STICS itself to the type of scenarios simulated is out of the scope of this paper. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Another factor that may influence the calibration procedure and the estimation of input quantities is the technical orientation of the different farm-types. More precisely, the existence of livestock may cause problems in the estimation of certain parameters used in the calculation of criterion λ , namely crop price p and the price of nitrogen cN . Specifically, p is estimated by summing the value of maize sales and the value of maize used on-farm, and then dividing by the total quantity of maize production reported. Since the value of maize used on-farm as animal feed is only an estimation of the implicit value (shadow price) of the crop, it might differ significantly from the "true" value that the farmer assigns to it in reality. Similarly, a large proportion of the nitrogen quantity used to grow crops in mainly animal producing farms may be of organic origin (animal manure) and therefore its "true" implicit value may not be adequately represented by the average price of mineral nitrogen considered in this paper. This issue can be addressed by enlarging the selected interval for cN when calculating the calibration criterion λ but the problem of erroneous implicit crop prices for animal producing farms requires considering different p values through simulation, instead of assuming a single point estimate. [SOURCE: Page_123]

Although in this paper we present an example of how the calibration procedure deals with a single crop, it can be applied to other irrigated crops and EU countries covered by FADN. It is thus obvious that further research is required to address the assumptions and sim- plifications that render it susceptible to various biases or sources of inaccuracy. For example, the simplification of considering the annual cost of water usage as a linear function of the total volume of irrigation water may not exactly correspond to the prevailing water pricing systems in certain regions (as in the case of increasing block tariffs). Another significant [SOURCE: Page_123]

123                                                         4.6. Discussion and Conclusions [SOURCE: Page_124]

drawback of the approach is that the estimated final water price cannot be subdivided into its different components (irrigation fees, technology costs and resource costs). Furthermore, irrigation efficiency in each farm is not addressed since the estimated water price does not provide information on the true financial cost of water. Estimating a farm-based irrigation efficiency index is also important for assessing the validity of the results, especially those regarding the actual amount of water used by a farm ; the water outputs of most crop models typically refer to the amount of water reaching the root zone and not what is actually used by the farmer (through pumping or drilling). Depending on the irrigation method (gravity, sprinkler or drip) and the condition of the irrigation network in the region, which are the key determinants of irrigation efficiency, the real amounts of water retrieved from water bodies and aquifers can be more than double what has been estimated in this exercise (Bos and Nugteren, 1974). [SOURCE: Page_124]

The above problems are the obvious trade-offs for a method that attempts to es- timate unobserved values of key economic and technical parameters of crop production under limited information. Nevertheless, most of these shortcomings can be addressed if the calibration problem is treated at farm and regional level, under specific assumptions regarding the irrigation technology and the prevailing water pricing systems. By doing so we can take advantage of all existing FADN information with the objective of estimating certain parameters that may be assumed to take similar values for all crops in a single farm or region. A typical example is the unit cost of electricity per amount of water pumped (technology cost of irrigation), which can be assumed to be the same for all irrigated crops on the same farm. Suffice it to say that having access to expert knowledge or other relevant databases is always beneficial, since additional constraints can improve the validity of the method. For instance, the spatially explicit information on the type of irrigation systems across the EU from the Land Use/Cover Area frame Statistical Survey (LUCAS) could also be used to make assumptions regarding the irrigation efficiency of farms belonging in a specific region. Finally, applying the calibration procedure at the regional level can also be seen as a way of [SOURCE: Page_124]

124                                                                                4.7. Appendix [SOURCE: Page_125]

assessing its validity by estimating the total resulting quantities of water for a specific region or country. [SOURCE: Page_125]

4.7      Appendix [SOURCE: Page_125]

4.7.1     Calibration criterion [SOURCE: Page_125]

FIGURE 4.5 – Illustration of the relationship between the Lagrange multiplier, represented here in the form of x (x-axis), and the level of collinearity between the two vectors (i.e. the price vector and the vector of the gradient to the yield function), expressed by the λ criterion (y-axis). [SOURCE: Page_125]

When nitrogen input is strictly positive, as it is usually the case, the calibration crite- rion developed in section 4.3.2 (equation 4.10) is expressed as : [SOURCE: Page_125]

[(cW − νW )cW + cN + (p + µ)p] λ = −  (cW − νW ) + cN + (p + µ) cW   + cN + p  [SOURCE: Page_125]

Criterion λ represents the collinearity between vector g, which corresponds to the gradient vector to the yield function when yield is given, and the price vector h. It is evident from the above equation that λ is a function of the Lagrange multiplier, µ, which results from the [SOURCE: Page_125]

125                                                                            4.7. Appendix [SOURCE: Page_126]

optimization program 4.4. In order to assess the behavior of the criterion value to variations of µ, we consider the case where water input is non zero (W ∗ >  and νW = ). The criterion λ can then be expressed as a function of x = p+µ p and c = cW + cN in the form :      [SOURCE: Page_126]

(c + x) λ = −  (c + ) (c + x ) [SOURCE: Page_126]

As presented in Figure 4.5, for any value of c and for x ≥  this function varies on the interval [, ] and indeed has a minimum of  when x = , i.e. µ =  (the two vectors are collinear). The behavior of the function supports the use of the criterion based on the geometrical approach, allowing us to measure the collinearity between both vectors and thus sort the MB functions estimated with the STICS crop model for selecting the best possible production function. [SOURCE: Page_126]

4.7.2    Farm typology [SOURCE: Page_126]

The farm-types of the AROPAj model are created using a clustering procedure for the farms in the FADN database, which is created using a survey on randomly selected farms in each EU country and reports more than 4000 variables for the surveyed farms. Because of the large sample size, the use of FADN data is simplified by creating farm-types. Each farm- type is in fact a virtual average farm, which is produced by averaging together farms with similar technical and economic characteristics. To preserve the anonymity of individual data, the European Comission mandates that each farm-type should gather at least 15 different sampled farms. The farm-types for Centre and Aquitaine French regions are presented in Table A$_1$. [SOURCE: Page_126]

Creating groups of similar farms require some subjectivity from the analyst, regarding the variables we consider and the weight assigned to each variable. We chose to use as a main variable for the clustering algorithm the Technical and Economic Orientation category [SOURCE: Page_126]

126                                                                                     4.7. Appendix [SOURCE: Page_127]

TABLE A$_1$ – Presentation of the farm-types created with the clustering procedure applied on individual farms in the FADN database. [SOURCE: Page_127]

herbivorous           300-600m         8.1        0-20% 19      36                   Mixed farming                     0-300m          8.2       20-40% 20      35           Arable crops and herbivorous              0-300m          7.7       20-40% 21      27               Poultry and pig farming               0-300m          8.0       20-40% 22      18                   Bovines (milk)                    0-300m          7.2        0-20% 23      18              Bovines (milk and meat)                0-300m          8.2        0-20% [SOURCE: Page_127]

(TEO), which is available in the FADN database and summarizes the production orientation of a surveyed farm. When less than 15 farms were found for a certain TEO, they were assigned to the closest TEO category, hence the subjectivity. The other variables considered, although with a smaller weight in the clustering algorithm, are altitude class, economic size class and mean irrigated area. [SOURCE: Page_127]

Chapitre 5 [SOURCE: Page_129]

Mise en oeuvre [SOURCE: Page_129]

128 [SOURCE: Page_129]

129                                                     5.1. Automatic calibration procedure [SOURCE: Page_130]

The previous sections are taken from a publication article focusing on two regions of France and one emblematic crop : maize. This chapter presents the method that has been developed in order to automatically calibrate the coupling between STICS and AROPAj for all crops and farm-types. The method, constructed to perform on every Member State of the UE-27, is tested on one country : France. [SOURCE: Page_130]

5.1      Automatic calibration procedure [SOURCE: Page_130]

5.1.1     The application [SOURCE: Page_130]

A schematic representation of the application is presented in Figure 5.1. It consists in a database with the necessary information required to generate the input files of the STICS crop model. R GNU is used to perform the regressions in order to retrieve the parameters of the Mitscherlich-Baule function presented in the previous chapter. [SOURCE: Page_130]

FIGURE 5.1 – Schematic representation of the automatic application used to create 3D response functions. [SOURCE: Page_130]

130                                                      5.1. Automatic calibration procedure [SOURCE: Page_131]

Built solely with free software, the application is set up to run on potentially any Linux operating system. It takes advantage of multicore capabilities of the machine to perform the simulations in parallel on the different processors. This option reduces the time required to generate the response functions by a factor greater than 10 (in the case of the medium sized server available in our laboratory, comprising 20 physical processors). [SOURCE: Page_131]

5.1.2     Varying irrigation amounts [SOURCE: Page_131]

The figure 5.2 displays the method we used to obtain yields for varying irrigation amounts. To achieve that, we perform a single simulation of the crop model with automatic irrigation and non limiting nitrogen inputs, corresponding to the upper value of the nitrogen range for each crop. The crop model is able to automatically adjust the irrigation events depending on the plant needs for the considered climate file. [SOURCE: Page_131]

After that step, the dates and irrigation amounts are stored in the database for the following simulations : the automatic irrigation option of STICS is turned off in the input file comprising the agricultural practices for the simulation. We use the same dates than for the initial simulation, but the irrigation amounts are reduced by a factor between 0 and 100 %. As shown in the figure, the consequence of reducing water inputs for each irrigation event is the accumulation of stress for the plant which affects yields. Visually, the orange area on the figure is correlated to the strength of the negative impacts on yields, which depends on the reduction factor applied to irrigation quantities. As soil water depletes, the stress increases and gets worse at the end of the growing season. [SOURCE: Page_131]

5.1.3     Estimating 3D response functions [SOURCE: Page_131]

The result is the generation of 36 points in the yield to nitrogen and water 3D space, as presented on figure 5.3. Each point corresponds to one STICS simulation. The main interest [SOURCE: Page_131]

131                                                     5.1. Automatic calibration procedure [SOURCE: Page_132]

FIGURE 5.2 – Methodology used to simulate the impact of varying total irrigation amounts on yields. The automatic irrigation option in STICS is used as the baseline (irrigation set to 100 %). For the next simulations, the irrigation events occur at the same date but less water is applied (40 % of baseline in this example). The plant progressively accumulate hydric stress, causing yield reductions. [SOURCE: Page_132]

132                                                            5.1. Automatic calibration procedure [SOURCE: Page_133]

of the method described in the previous section is the possibility to estimate a range of water amounts corresponding to total water quantities for the crop. Other methods using varying levels of stress to activate irrigation could be used (this is an option of the crop model). However, this would generate nonlinear total quantites of water and complicate the summarizing in the form of a function of total water amounts. [SOURCE: Page_133]

The nonlinear regression used exhibited great performance, most of the time with R superior to 90 %. This shows that, according to the model, the response of the yields to total irrigation water is similar to the exponential shape of the Mitscherlich-Baule function. Other types of function such as polynomial functions would also show good performance on this type of data. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Profit equation. [SOURCE: Page_133]

Selection criterion (µ). [SOURCE: Page_133]

Best function for Maize in region « Centre ». [SOURCE: Page_133]

FIGURE 5.3 – STICS simulation outputs : each point represent a single STICS simulation for varying water and nitrogen input levels for maize in region Centre. The surface shows the corresponding regression, using the Mitscherlich-Baule function. [SOURCE: Page_133]

133                                                                     5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_134]

5.2      Application to France [SOURCE: Page_134]

We have been able to use this aplication to calibrate the STICS-AROPAj coupling for France. The following section focuses on an analysis of the results of this calibration, at national and regional levels. Comparisons with actual data is used to validate our approach. [SOURCE: Page_134]

5.2.1    Regional coding [SOURCE: Page_134]

The figure 5.4 shows the FADN regional coding at the European level. A unique ID number is assigned to each region, corresponding to the NUTS II level in the FADN database (for most countries). As this numbering will be used in the presentation of the results of the calibration and future simulations from now on, we can use this map as a reference. [SOURCE: Page_134]

As the focus will be on France, we also provide the administrative name of the consi- dered regions for this country in the following table (table A$_1$) : [SOURCE: Page_134]

135                                                                 5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_136]

5.2.2     Selected input prices [SOURCE: Page_136]

The calibration procedure provides with an estimation of the cost of access (input "price", cw and cN ) to both inputs considered in the dose-response functions generated by the crop model, from a range of potential values. This was necessary because for nitrogen, even though annual national estimations are available, fertilizer prices are correlated to energy prices which exhibit monthly variations, meaning it is more realistic to allow for some flexibility around the average value. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Regarding water, actual input price is even harder to obtain as regional and local infor- mation is often not available. Therefore, additional costs to use and maintain the irrigation systems are paid by farmers, and those costs are even more difficult to retrieve. Moreo- ver, the provenance of water in different regions can widely differ (groudwater pumping vs distribution network) making the data on actual water price very hard to obtain. The automatic procedure using the calibration criterion λ was constructed to deal with this lack of information. [SOURCE: Page_136]

Figure 5.5 displays the national distribution of selected input prices. Regarding ni- trogen, most selected prices were between 1 and 2 euros per kilo of unitary nitrogen. The peak of the distribution is close to the average value of the reference year for a standard fertilizer. Higher nitrogen prices are due to the impact of the nitrogen fraction contained in the fertilizers associated to each crop. Indeed, different types of fertilizers contain various percentage of nitrogen, and our data was constructed using an average fertilizer for each crop. For example, some crops such as potato require on average the use of more expensive nitrogen (when both the price and the nitrogen content of the fertilizer were accounted). [SOURCE: Page_136]

For water, the range of potential values was 0.02 to 0.20 euros per cubic meter. Saturation toward lower values of water price are probably due to the large amount of non irrigated crops in France, for which irrigation amounts are close to zero and the functions [SOURCE: Page_136]

136                                                                                5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_137]

Water price (euro per m^3)               Nitrogen price (euro per kilo of nitrogen) [SOURCE: Page_137]

Frequency [SOURCE: Page_137]

0.05          0.10         0.15      0.20 1          2             3            4           5 Selected price per input, in euros [SOURCE: Page_137]

FIGURE 5.5 – Input prices retrieved from the automatic calibration procedure at the national level. [SOURCE: Page_137]

can be interpreted as two-dimensional nitrogen-to-yield response functions (see Godard et al. (2008)). [SOURCE: Page_137]

This result is detailed in the figure 5.6. Even though the effect is still present for most crops, we note that irrigated crops are often associated to higher values of water price, causing the distribution to flatten (maize, potato and sunflower in particular). The absence of a distribution peak is certainly due to regional heterogeneity in the cost of access to water. The fact that little saturation to the highest value of the range (20 cents per cubic meter) indicate that first, the calibration was able to successfully find combinations of water prices and response functions (each data point comes from the best possible combination, calculated using the calibration criterion) and second, that the range of values is likely to be realistic. [SOURCE: Page_137]

137                                                                                5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_138]

Wheat                          Durum wheat                     Beetroot [SOURCE: Page_138]

Rapeseed                           Maize                         Barley Frequency [SOURCE: Page_138]

0.05    0.10     0.15    0.20   0.05     0.10     0.15   0.20   0.05    0.10     0.15   0.20 Selected water price in euro per m^3 [SOURCE: Page_138]

FIGURE 5.6 – Distribution of water prices at the national level, for each crop. [SOURCE: Page_138]

138                                                                5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_139]

Missing information is one of the key points making this type of study difficult, i.e. inte- grating biological information into an economic model for a large geographical zone. Using ranges of potential values enable to work around this issue. At the national level, the average water price paid by farmers was 6.84 cents per cubic meter, while nitrogen price was 1.92 euros per kilo of nitrogen. [SOURCE: Page_139]

5.2.3    Optimal water input per crop and farm-type [SOURCE: Page_139]

With the calibration method developed in the previous sections, we automatically in- fer optimal water and nitrogen inputs according to available economic and yield information, for each crop in each farm-type. Figure 5.7 shows the estimated water use in the y-axis per crop and farm-type, that is the land area dedicated to each crop multiplied by the optimal water input W ∗ resulting from the calibration. The x-axis in this graph shows the irrigated areas for each crop and each farm-type, information available for the major crops in the FADN database but not used directly in the calibration. [SOURCE: Page_139]

This figure thus displays the average water use per hectare in each crop/farm-type combination ; indeed, the dashed black line corresponds to an application of 1000 m^3 of irrigation water per hectare. To help with the vizualisation, a linear regression was added to the figure (blue line). [SOURCE: Page_139]

Irrigated area and water use are proportional in a majority of cases, illustrating the good performance of the calibration procedure. For maize, the linear regression consistently indicates water applications superior to 1000 m3 per hectare, which is expected as maize is one of the crop that requires the most irrigation. The same result is obtained for other crops such as soy, wheat and potato. This is consistent with data on irrigation practices in France. Interstingly, the calibration suggests that rapeseed and sunflower necessite less water input, and we know that these crops are often less irrigated. In the field, sunflower only requires 2 to 3 irrigation applications events depending on the climate (each application typically [SOURCE: Page_139]

The intuitions from maize in Centre and Aquitain regions presented previously can thus be generalized at the national level for each crop considered by the model. [SOURCE: Page_141]

The following figure 5.8 aims at illustrating the role of the farm-type typology developed in this thesis. FIGURE 5.8 – Calibrated quantities of irrigation water per farm-type and region according to agricultural areas. The dashed line corresponds to 1000 m^3 per hectare, while the size of each point refers to the percentage of irrigated agricultural area according to the typology of farm-types. [SOURCE: Page_141]

This figure shows in the x-axis the sum of agricultural areas for each farm-type, while the y-axis represents the sum of irrigation water added on all crops of the farm-type, accor- ding to the calibration. Each point is a farm-type, with the size of the point being proportional [SOURCE: Page_141]

141                                                                  5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_142]

to the percentage of irrigated area in each farm-type, a variable used in the typology. As before, the dashed black line corresponds to water applications of 1000 m^3 per hectare. [SOURCE: Page_142]

In most cases, water use per hectare is largely below 1000 m^3 per hectare, which is expected as this figure represents not only irrigated areas but all agricultural areas, including rainfed crops. However, we observe that the most water consuming farm-types, i.e. the ones closer to the dashed line, are usually the most irrigated according to the typology, represented in the figure by the wide circles. [SOURCE: Page_142]

This means that the calibration automatically assigns to the irrigated farm-types response functions for which the economic optimum was obtained with application of larger quantities of irrigation water. Differences among farm-types are in part due to the fact that each farm-type in each region is subject to various climates, in addition to the different agricultural productions requiring varying amounts of water. This could be the explanation why it is difficult to obtain a perfect relation between percentage of irrigated area and the two variables used to create this figure : see for example the smaller circle inside a wider circle at 1000 m^3 per hectare in region 152, among other "inconsistencies". Still, these results justify the relevance of using the irrigation variable to create the typology of farm-types (along with the type of production, altitude and economic size). [SOURCE: Page_142]

5.2.4    National and regional water use for irrigation [SOURCE: Page_142]

Figure 5.9 displays the irrigation water usage in each farm-type in present climate. This enables to vizualise which and how much the different crops are irrigated. [SOURCE: Page_142]

Overall, the results are in line with what is known about French agricultural systems. In the south of France, regions 164 to 203, most water is applied to maize (represented in orange). In the north of France, potato, durum wheat and rapeseed are the main irrigated crops. Some water is also applied to maize in some regions such as Centre (134), Alsace (152) [SOURCE: Page_142]

142                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_143]

and Poitou-Charentes (162), but as less area is dedicated to this crop in the north, so water quantities are obviously less remarkable. This does not mean that less quantities of water per hectare are applied. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Finally, figure 5.10 displays regional water quantities for irrigation according to the model (in blue). These resulting quantities can be compared with regional data from the french ministry of agriculture in grey in the figure for the year 2009. [SOURCE: Page_143]

Results from the calibration appear to be quite close to official data in most cases. In [SOURCE: Page_143]

143                                                                                                           5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_144]

the very south of France (regions 203-PACA, 201-Languedoc-Roussilon) the water quantities are much lower, but this is easily explained by the fact that horticultural productions are not represented in the economic model and represent a large part of the agricultural production in these regions, with generalized irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_144]

In the other most irrigated regions (134-Centre, 182-Aquitaine and 183-Midi-Pyrénées), we were able to retrieve the right order of magnitude of irrigation quantities. Overestima- tions, particularly for region Centre, may be in part due to the fact that some water withdra- wals are hidden to the government, in the form of local pumping stations run by farmers or group of farmers to pump water from the aquifers. [SOURCE: Page_144]

FIGURE 5.10 – Regional irrigation water consumption in comparison with official data from the french ministry of agriculture for the calibrated year 2009. [SOURCE: Page_144]

In the north of France the model tend to estimate higher volumes of water for ir- rigation than the official data, see regions 121, 131, 132 and 141 in particular. The same argument than for region Centre could be used, but the previous figure may suggest that the calibration for some crops is not accurate. For example, in Figure 5.9, the farm-types in these [SOURCE: Page_144]

144                                                                5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_145]

regions apply very large amounts of water to certain crops (Potato in farm-type 13 of region 132, Rapeseed in farm-types 19 and 20 in region Centre) which is probably inconsistent with what actually happens in these regions. [SOURCE: Page_145]

Still, using mostly economic and yield data, the method based on response functions from a crop model generates realistic estimations of water quantities at the aggregated level. Indeed, the sum of irrigation water just above 3 billion cubic meter predicted by the model is coherent with the national average for the calibrated year 2009 (between 3 to 4 billion depending on the source). In the following chapters, this calibrated year will constitute the baseline on which the impact of future climates will be derived. [SOURCE: Page_145]

To conclude this section, we have demonstrated that the coupling of the STICS crop model and the AROPAj economic model, via the use of water-and-nitrogen response functions, enables us to represent the consumption of water for irrigation. National and regional ag- gregates of water quantities are consistent with national data. Moreover, focusing on each crop individually suggests that the actual agricultural practices could be represented in this manner. [SOURCE: Page_145]

5.2.5     Impact of water price [SOURCE: Page_145]

The calibration procedure developed in this chapter enables the modeler to retrieve the cost of access of each input from a range of potential values. This is useful when the data is not available which is especially the case for water, and justified the use of a range of potential values in the calibration as explained previously in this chapter. This section provides an analysis of the impact of varying cost of access to water at the national level. Modifying the water costs is the simplest way to simulate a variation of the stress on water resource, which is shared by the agricultural systems with other sectors. This section is thus a sensitivity analysis on water price parameters. The access cost of water will be called water [SOURCE: Page_145]

145                                                                                                      5.2. Application to France [SOURCE: Page_146]

price for simplicity. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Method [SOURCE: Page_146]

Estimated water prices are different for each crop and each farm-type. In the following analysis, a multiplication coefficient was applied uniformely to all water price data for France. The selected range was -50 % to +50 % of base price, per step of 10 %. Simulations were performed using present climate data for the year 2009. [SOURCE: Page_146]

Results [SOURCE: Page_146]

Figure 5.11 shows the impact of varying water prices at the national scale (France). The calibrated total added irrigation water was around 3000 million cubic meter for the reference year (relative price equal to 1.0). [SOURCE: Page_146]

At the national scale, the AROPAj model with three dimensional response functions is able to represent the adaptation of farmers to varying water prices. A decrease in water inputs per hectare, or intensive margin, is likely to be a major component of this behavior as we did not observe major changes in land area dedicated to the various represented crops. This is expected as we only modified the water price ; a change in climate would have more impact on agricultural areas. Interestingly, we can note that the impact of varying water prices has notable consequences on the gross margin of the agricultural sector represented by the model. The results also showed that some regional heterogenity is observed in the response of the model to varying water prices. [SOURCE: Page_149]

149                                                                           5.3. Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_150]

5.3     Conclusion [SOURCE: Page_150]

In this chapter, we applied the calibration method developed in the previous chapter in order to calibrate the coupling between the STICS crop model and the AROPAj econo- mic model for the reference year 2009, through the use of Mitscherlich-Baule response functions. We showed that the calibration procedure produces realistic results regarding water quantities and input prices, at the aggregated national level but also at the regional and farm-type level. [SOURCE: Page_150]

The next step is to deform the response functions in future climate, in order to predict the impacts of climate change on French agricultural systems. [SOURCE: Page_150]

Chapitre 6 [SOURCE: Page_151]

Impacts du changement climatique [SOURCE: Page_151]

150 [SOURCE: Page_151]

151                                                                             6.1. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_152]

Following the calibration procedure developed in the previous chapters, the modeling tool created enables to study the impacts of climate change. In this chapter, the response of agricultural systems of France to climate change are presented for the 2045-2055 decade. [SOURCE: Page_152]

6.1     Introduction [SOURCE: Page_152]

Climate is a major element of the productivity of farming systems. In future climate scenarios, the shift of average climate variables, such as increases in temperatures or changes in precipitation levels, will affect the whole agricultural sector. In order to study the potential consequences of climate change, calibrated response functions showing the response of yield to agricultural inputs can be re-estimated in the new climate conditions, before providing these functions to the economic model. [SOURCE: Page_152]

Climate interannual variability is a key element of climate. The introduction emphasi- zed the strong potential effects that such events can have on agriculture. To take into account this effect, using several climate years resulting from climate modeling is an important first step regarding this issue ; this principle has to be compared with the use of average climates which can not capture these effects. [SOURCE: Page_152]

The AROPAj economic model represents both the animal and vegetal productions of farming systems. The following variables can thus be studied in this chapter : gross margin and changes in land allocation, production, fertilizer use and greenhouse gas emissions. In addition, water needs for irrigation will also be displayed for the different yearly climates. [SOURCE: Page_152]

The fine spatial and temporal representation of the modeling tools, comprising regio- nal farm-types as well as daily climate data for each region, permits to capture the outputs of farming systems for each region and farm-type with great accuracy, at least up to the regional level. This is relevant because of the heterogeneity of farming systems in France, as well as because the various local climates observed in these agricultural regions. Some [SOURCE: Page_152]

152                                                                             6.2. Method [SOURCE: Page_153]

elements of analysis will thus be focused at the regional scale. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Finally, when considering longer term horizons, the modeler should include in the analysis the capacity to use new agricultural practices. These new practices can consist in the use of new crop species or changes in the crop rotations. Moreover, increases in temperature in 2050 can cause shift in agricultural practices from the southern latitudes to higher latitudes as an adaptation mechanism. Sowing dates and the length of the growing period can also be affected by higher temperature and CO fertilization in the future (see Leclère et al. (2013) for seminal work dedicated to the analysis of autonomous adaptation of farming systems). [SOURCE: Page_153]

In this result chapter, simulations without adaptation are first presented, meaning climate is the only difference with the reference year 2009. Then, a section will focus on the role of adaptation of new agricultural practices and its potential consequences in comparison with the scenarios without adaptation. Regarding irrigation water, a sensitivity analysis of the response to changes in water prices in future climate and its consequence in terms of gross margin and irrigation quantities is also provided. [SOURCE: Page_153]

6.2     Method [SOURCE: Page_153]

6.2.1    Future climate [SOURCE: Page_153]

The 3 climate scenarios used in the simulations are SRES A1b, A$_2$ and B$_1$. These scenarios are based on various socio-economic scenarios of development at the global scale. The focus of this section will be dedicated to the 2050 horizon, with the climate years 2045 to 2055 being considered. Climate data are constructed using the ARPEGE climate model. [SOURCE: Page_153]

The different hypothesis on which these scenarios are constructed lead to different levels of CO concentrations. Figure 6.1 displays CO concentrations in ppm between 2000 [SOURCE: Page_153]

153                                                                                                      6.2. Method [SOURCE: Page_154]

and 2100. For the decade we consider, A1b and A$_2$ exhibit the higher concentrations, bet- ween 520 and 550 ppm, while B$_1$ is the most optimistic scenarios with concentrations under 550 ppm. [SOURCE: Page_154]

800 CO$_2$ concentration (ppm) [SOURCE: Page_154]

FIGURE 6.1 – Evolution of future CO concentrations for three climate scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_154]

A representation of other important climatic variables, namely daily maximum and minimum temperatures and average daily precipitations in France, are shown in figure 6.2 in the form of trimestrial averages. Temperatures are higher for A1b and A$_2$ than for B$_1$ due to more radiation forcing caused by higher CO concentrations. Temperatures exhibit moderate levels of interannual variability for trimestrial averages. [SOURCE: Page_154]

Precipitation levels present more interannual variability. On average, according to climate models more water would available around winter (JFM and OND) while in spring and summer (AMJ and JAS) less rainwater would be available compared to present climate or at least in our reference year. [SOURCE: Page_154]

It is important to note that at a more local scale the climate can deviate from these national averages. [SOURCE: Page_154]

155                                                                               6.2. Method [SOURCE: Page_156]

6.2.2    Deformation of response functions [SOURCE: Page_156]

To study the impact of climate change, we run the STICS crop model using the cali- brated response functions (each corresponding to the selection of a crop species, soil and agricultural practices for each crop in each farm-type), while changing the climate data. [SOURCE: Page_156]

Figure 6.3 displays variations of two key paramaters of the Mitscherlich-Baule res- ponse functions. Each year of the decade 2045-2055 of A$_2$ climatic scenario is showed. The results presented in these graphs are expressed in variation with the calibrated parameters of the functions in present climate, for each crop associated with response functions in the economic model. [SOURCE: Page_156]

We observe that modifying the climate has a notable impact on maximum yields. The STICS crop model simulates the growth of each crop for a specific climate file ; many variables such as temperatures or precipitations have a strong impact on agricultural production. The deformed response functions are obtained via a regression on 36 crop model outputs corresponding to 6 nitrogen input values and 6 irrigation water percentages in the same way than for the calibration in present climate. Annual variability is relatively high, depending on the crop. It seems that in this climatic scenario maize yields tend to be higher than present climate yields. [SOURCE: Page_156]

The W parameter is correlated to natural available water through rain, and is ex- pressed in cubic meter per hectare. High interannual variabilities for this parameter are expected as irrigation demand is very dependant on precipitation amounts and dates. This variability is the main reason for which several climatic years were used. We can expect the total irrigation demand of the agricultural sector to vary substantially between each year. Comparing averages for the different climate scenarios will also be possible, in order to assess the potential impact of climate change on water demand and yields, among other variables. [SOURCE: Page_156]

These deformed functions are then used as inputs for the AROPAj economic model, which will perform economic adjustments in response to this new environmental context (climate change). [SOURCE: Page_158]

6.3      Results [SOURCE: Page_158]

6.3.1    Impacts of future climate : 2050 horizon [SOURCE: Page_158]

Main results [SOURCE: Page_158]

The following results correspond to simulations for the 2050 decade (from 2045 to 2055) in the form of an average for France. The three climate scenarios considered were SRES A1b, B$_1$ and A$_2$. The simulations were performed without adaptation of the agricul- tural practices (no change in crop species nor sowing dates). However, the economic model performed an optimization resulting in changes in input levels, water and nitrogen, as well as changes of land allocation and number of animals for cattle farms. [SOURCE: Page_158]

Figure 6.4 shows the effect of climate change on four main output variables of the AROPAj model : gross margin, total applied irrigation water or water consumption, CO -eq GHG emissions and fertilizer utilization. [SOURCE: Page_158]

In these simulations, all future climates showed a decrease in gross margin, GHG emissions and fertilizer consumption. Table A$_1$ indicates the corresponding numerical values. Gross margin losses reached 937, 632 and 402 million euros for scenarios A1b, A$_2$ and B$_1$ respectively. CO -eq emissions decreased by 3 Mt for scenario A1b and around 2 Mt for A$_2$ and B$_1$, in correlation with the reduction of fertilizer use. [SOURCE: Page_158]

On average, all three scenarios showed an increase in water consumption particularly [SOURCE: Page_158]

FIGURE 6.7 – Distribution of changes in water consumption for the decade 2045-2055 in comparison with the reference year 2009. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Figure 6.7 shows the same result for the total water consumption of the agricultural sector. More variation is observed for this variable with some climatic years causing a dou- bling of water use for irrigation. This is particularly true for the A$_2$ scenario. Most years for the other two scenarios are quite close to the baseline : the distribution peak corresponds to water use of 105% and 110% if the baseline year for A1b and B$_1$ scenarios respecti- vely. However, these distributions are skewed towards higher values of water consumption, explaining why average water use presented previously is higher for all simulated future climates. [SOURCE: Page_161]

Regional water use [SOURCE: Page_161]

Regarding water use, the figure 6.8 shows the yearly regional water use in future climates as well as the baseline values for the reference year 2009. Some regions show a re- [SOURCE: Page_161]

161                                                                                6.3. Results [SOURCE: Page_162]

gular increase in water consumption in future climate (132-Picardie, 135-Basse-Normandie, 136-Bourgogne, 162-Poitou-Charentes or 163-Bretagne). These regions are mostly nor- thern regions ; the south of France appears to exhibit less increase in irrigation use with even reductions of water consumption for regions 182-Aquitaine or 183-Midi-Pyrénées for example. [SOURCE: Page_162]

Interestingly, a high interannual and regional variability is observed, which was not obvious from the aggregated data. This was expected as irrigation quantities are highly climate dependant : interannual variability in amount and dates of precipitation events can strongly impact the need to provide additional water during the key moments of plant growth ; this is true in any climate period and is due to the high variability in precipitations patterns. [SOURCE: Page_162]

However, the figure shows that some climatic years had a particularly strong impact on regional water use. See year 2046 for climate A1b and region 183-Midi-Pyrénées or year 2048 for climate a^2 and region 134-Centre for example, among others. In these cases, a doubling of water use is observed, which is particularly notable as these regions are highly irrigated. These results can explain the shape of the distribution of water usage presented previously in figure 6.7. [SOURCE: Page_162]

These result are very heterogenous both spatially (between regions) and temporally (between climatic years). This illustrates the importance of having a model that can capture the regional diversity of both climate and farming systems, as well as using various future climatic years as opposed to an average climate, especially when considering irrigation water. [SOURCE: Page_162]

FIGURE 6.9 – National average areas and agricultural productions for the 3 climate scenarios and for the reference year (REF). [SOURCE: Page_164]

Figure 6.9 shows the average areas and productions for the 4 considered climatic periods. Regarding land allocation, we observe an increase in fodder areas in future climate. This increase is balanced by a decrease in land allocated to agricultural productions, wheat in particular. [SOURCE: Page_164]

The consequences are a decrease in agricultural production, especially for scenario A1b and also A2, while B1 is less affected with levels of production comparable with the reference. Scenario A1b was marked by a decrease of around 10,000 thousand tons in total agricultural output, compared to the 120,000 thousand tons for the reference year. The [SOURCE: Page_164]

164                                                                                  6.3. Results [SOURCE: Page_165]

negative impact of climate seems particularly strong on wheat production. [SOURCE: Page_165]

Scenario B$_1$ appears to be the less damaging on productions, with a notable increase in beetroot production. Beetroot is a very productive crop with yields often over 100 tons per hectare, meaning that if we consider only cereals the production is also negatively impacted in this scenario. [SOURCE: Page_165]

Figure 6.10 presents a focus on the annual variation in land allocation and production for 4 major crops. Production of barley and wheat follow closely the changes in land allocation, meaning that changes in production are in part due to extensive effects. For wheat, the higher magnitude of the changes in production, which decrease down to 60% of reference production, indicates the existence of a negative intensive effect on yields. [SOURCE: Page_165]

The situation for maize is different : this crop interestingly exhibits lower areas dedi- cated to this crop, while production tends to increase, up to 40% for some particular years (2049 for A$_2$ and 2050 for A1b). This reveals an intensive effect with higher yields in a res- ponse to the future climate. Potato on the contrary shows relatively similar areas in future climate, while productions are consistently lower. In some cases, a major drop in production is observed, see year 2051 and 2052. [SOURCE: Page_165]

To quantify the intensive effect of climate, figure 6.11 displays the average national yield for 6 major crops for each simulated climatic year. As expected, wheat shows a notable decrease in yields, mainly for A$_2$ and A1b scenarios. Rapeseed is also negatively affected. The same result is observed for barley, even though yield losses are limited for the A$_2$ scenario. [SOURCE: Page_165]

Two crops present major increase in yields : maize and beetroot. Consistently, maize production is 1 to 2 tons superior in futures climate, sometimes even reaching 10 tons per hectare (2049-A2 and 2050-A1b) in comparison with the national average just above 7 tons in 2009. This explains the previous results : for maize, the decrease in land allocation is largely [SOURCE: Page_165]

6.3.2    Impacts of changes in water price in future climate [SOURCE: Page_168]

Previous results were created with solely changes in climatic data. The following section consists in the same procedure with the addition of an analysis on the impact on varying water prices. The selected range of prices is the following : -50%, -25%, +25% and +50% of the reference prices. The intent is to simulate the impact of changes in the constraint on water resource in France which as explained in the previous chapters is use for diverse sectors, including agriculture. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Figure 6.12 shows the impact of varying water prices on the distributions of agricultural gross margin and irrigation amounts for the 3 future climate scenarios. Similarly than for present climate, changes in water prices provoke a translation of the distributions. The impact obviously appears to be more pronounced for total irrigation water, the most sensitive variable to water price. [SOURCE: Page_168]

In the scenario characterized with a 50% reduction in water price, several years showed total irrigation values for France above 7 billion cubic meters which constitue a doubling of present climate water consumption (around 3 to 4 billion cubic meters). This is especially the case for A$_2$ scenario. On the contrary, increases of water price to double its present value causes the distribution of water demand to shift to lower water consumption comparable with present climate data, at around 3 billion cubic meters. [SOURCE: Page_168]

Table A2 shows averaged values corresponding to the previous figure 6.13. We ob- serve variations in gross margin of the order of 1% for the largest changes in water price (plus or minus 50%). A 50% increase in water price causes average losses of 165, 186 and [SOURCE: Page_168]

The figure 6.13 displays the consequences of varying water prices on average yields for 6 crops. Wheat, barley, rapeseed and potato are little affected, with the exception of the years 2051 and 2052 for wheat in the A$_2$ scenario. These two climatic years are characterized by subpar yields in comparison with the other years, suggesting the advent of a climatic event having a strong negative impact on this crop. FIGURE 6.13 – Yearly variation of the national average yield for 6 major crops in future climate with varying water prices. [SOURCE: Page_171]

The two crops whose yields were most affected by water prices are maize and bee- troot, two highly irrigated crops. For maize, our simulations suggest that the yield response [SOURCE: Page_171]

171                                                                               6.3. Results [SOURCE: Page_172]

to varying water prices to be relatively independant of the climatic year, as can be seen from the vertical translations of yields in figure. Beetroot exhibits more variation in yields in reaction to changing water prices : in the year 2048 for A$_2$ for example, a decrease in water prices by 50% permitted to obtain much higher yields than in the other water price scenarios, meaning that, for this climatic year, water input probably was the limiting factor for production. Changes in available water from precipitations during the critical growing periods of the crop are probably the main cause of this result. This effect is not observed in some other years, meaning that other climatic variables such as temperature were probably the cause of the observed interannual variation in yields (see year 2045 for B$_1$ and A$_2$ for example). [SOURCE: Page_172]

6.3.3    Role of adaptation [SOURCE: Page_172]

The previous section consisted in predictions of impacts of future climates on agricul- tural systems with economic adaptation i.e. changes in land allocation and input quantities. However, adaptation of agricultural practices are known to play a major role in the mitigation of negative effects of climate change. In this section, following the work of Leclère et al. (2013), we used STICS crop model simulations to capture this effect. [SOURCE: Page_172]

Method [SOURCE: Page_172]

In order to do so, we used the calibrated response functions in present climate, each function corresponding to one type of soil, one sowing date and one variety (for each crop in each farm-type). Then, we performed simulations in future climate for alternative varieties and sowing dates, while assuming the type of soil to be the same. Varieties used were the one present in the STICS standard files in the same manner than for the calibration. In these simulations, creation of hypothetical high-performing crop varieties was not considered (GMOs, drought resistant crops etc...), meaning that the main effect we can capture is the [SOURCE: Page_172]

172                                                                                    6.3. Results [SOURCE: Page_173]

use of typical "southern" varieties in higher latitudes due to temperature increases. [SOURCE: Page_173]

For sowing dates we used the following range of values around the calibrated date for present climate : -28 days, -14 days, same day, +14 days and +28 days. This relatively wide range is based on the concept that in future climate, changes in climate variables will affect the growing seasons of crops, with optimal sowing dates which may be rather different than present day practices. In addition, total growing period tend to decrease with increases in temperature, meaning that the next crop in the rotation can potentially be sowed earlier, which is another effect affecting agricultural practices. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Results [SOURCE: Page_173]

Table A$_3$ displays averaged output of the economic model simulations with and without adaptation of agricultural practices around 2050. Compared with the simulations without adaptation, we observe a notable increase in gross margin, irrigation water, GHG emissions and fertilizer consumption. Interestingly, it appears that the scale of the changes is higher than the variations between the climatic scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_173]

TABLE A$_3$ – Main results in future climate with and without adaptation. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Climate   Adaptation    Gross margin       Irrigation water       CO -eq emissions   Fertilizer consumption (million euros)   (million cubic meters)          (Mt)                   (Mt) present       No         28151.70               3027.93                 94.17                   8.50 a^1b           Yes        30169.30               7281.34                 93.15                   8.69 a^1b           No         27215.19               3776.71                 91.09                   7.29 a^2            Yes        30462.08               7035.28                 93.97                   9.01 a^2            No         27519.73               4370.63                 91.87                   7.55 b^1            Yes        31006.46               7433.57                 94.54                   9.36 b^1            No         27749.81               3701.43                 92.19                   7.77 [SOURCE: Page_173]

Figure 6.14 focus on the percentage change in gross margin with or without adapta- tion, in the form of probability distributions. While sole climate change had a negative impact on economic output of the sector, adaptation turned around the situation. In every climatic scenario, gross margin could potentially be 5% to 15% higher than in present climate. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Results for agricultural productions are presented in figure 6.17. Logically, adaptation caused production increases due to larger agricultural areas dedicated to cereals and other types of productions. National productions are then similar or slightly higher than present day productions (wheat for example). However, maize production is much higher with adaptation than any other simulations with a doubling of present day production levels. [SOURCE: Page_175]

Since maize areas are similar than in present climate, this means a major increase in yields per hectare (including on-farm consumption). Figure 6.18 shows the national average [SOURCE: Page_175]
